# Changelog

## Unreleased

- **Image signature check names the renamed repository.** Release images are signed by `zyvorai/zyvor-netra`'s workflow since the repository rename, so the `cosign verify` command in `deploy/README.md` (which named `zyvorai/netra`) failed; it now matches the signer. Clone commands, Helm chart `home`/`sources`, docs-site and README links point at `zyvorai/zyvor-netra` too (the Go module path is unchanged).
- **README maturity table covers 0.30.0.** L7 names and query types, per-second metrics, node isolation (loaded but inert until a policy is set) and security review now say what runs by default.
- **`web-e2e` checks every shipped page again.** Its page list lacked `metrics`, `metric-anomalies`, `metric-alerts` and `security-review`, so the job failed on its own consistency check since the metrics platform landed.

## 0.30.0 — 2026-10-07

- **Security intelligence and review.** Matched DNS response events carry the query type (`dnsQType`, two bytes of existing event padding, so the 196-byte event ABI and every earlier offset are unchanged), which turns on the DNS detector's `txt-heavy` signal. The threat feed gains canonical indicators, an optional `?ttl=`, `X-Netra-Intel-Revision` compare-and-set, the last 16 revisions with rollback (`GET /api/v1/intel/history`, `POST /api/v1/intel/rollback/{revision}`, `netractl intel history|rollback`), an optional persistent journal (`NETRA_INTEL_STATE_PATH`) and a leader-only HTTPS refresh (`NETRA_INTEL_SOURCE_URL`) that keeps the last good revision on any bad download. `GET /api/v1/security/optimizer` reports equivalent, covered and overlapping deny predicates, and `GET /api/v1/security/incidents` correlates DNS findings, scan findings and feed hits on the same exact workload within ten minutes (`netractl security optimizer|incidents`, **Security → Security Review**, read-only MCP tools `netra_security_optimizer`, `netra_security_incidents`, `netra_intel_history`). None of it applies a deny, deletes a rule or changes mode; applying a feed still needs a lease and `X-Netra-Confirm-Risk: high`. [Docs](docs/security-review.md)
- **MCP tool counts corrected.** `netra-mcp` registers 189 tools (129 read, 60 mutating); README, capabilities, MCP integration, brochure and website copy said 180 or 183, and one place said 52 mutating.
- **L7/DNS programs load on Linux 6.8+ again.** `netra_l7_cgroup_egress` was rejected by the verifier on the lab host (Linux 7.0) with "the sequence of 8193 jumps is too complex", in the image built before this change as well, so `NETRA_L7=auto` had been running those hosts without TLS SNI, HTTP Host, DNS query names or DNS QTYPE. The SNI and Host searches indexed packet memory with their loop counters, so the verifier tracked one state per iteration until its branch queue passed the fixed 8192 limit; with that lifted, the unrolled DNS QNAME decoder alone exceeded the 1,000,000-instruction budget. The parsers in `bpf/netra_l7.h` now copy up to 640 payload bytes with `bpf_skb_load_bytes` into a work area in the per-CPU scratch map and walk it with `bpf_loop` callbacks (nested for name copies), keeping parser state in the map so the verifier treats it as unknown. `netra_l7_cgroup_egress` now verifies in about 95,000 instructions and `netra_l7_cgroup_ingress` in about 37,000 (Linux 7.0, clang 18 as in `Dockerfile.agent`; clang 21 also loads). Behavior is unchanged: positions searched, name rules and outputs match, the existing parser tests pass on the new API, and new ones cover an invalid SNI candidate before the real one, Host after other headers with tabs, a Host value ending the payload, and a QNAME cut by a compression pointer. The parsers now also see bytes outside the skb's linear area, and the HTTP method is read from the same copy: egress request bytes usually sit outside it, so reading the method from packet memory had missed most requests. Two fixes found while testing live: a DNS response is now matched to its query by the receiving socket's cgroup (`bpf_skb_cgroup_id`) rather than `bpf_get_current_cgroup_id()`, which on ingress is whatever task the softirq interrupted, so no response had ever matched (node-wide `dnsHealth` showed 320 queries and 0 responses); responses, latency, RCODE and QTYPE now arrive. Live on the lab host after deploy: `www.wikipedia.org` SNI and `neverssl.com` `GET` Host recorded, DNS responses matched (284 on the node within a minute), and TXT responses carrying QTYPE 16 with RCODE 3 for a nonexistent name. `bpf_loop` needs Linux 5.17; older kernels drop the L7 programs through `NETRA_L7=auto` as before. Docs: `docs/l7-metadata.md`.
- **Per-second metrics platform.** Every agent now collects host (CPU, memory, pressure, disks, filesystems), network stack (interfaces, SNMP/netstat, sockstat, softnet, conntrack), cgroup v2 (one chart per pod; per container with `NETRA_METRICS_CGROUP_CONTAINERS=true`), comm-only process groups, eBPF datapath and per-workload RED series once a second into a Gorilla-compressed store (`internal/tsdb`), and streams them to netrad over a replay buffer so a restart or network blip leaves no gap. netrad keeps 1 s for an hour, 1 min for two weeks and 1 h for a year per node. Every dimension gets an unsupervised k-means anomaly model (`internal/anomaly`, stdlib only), with anomaly rates on every query and a Kolmogorov–Smirnov "what changed here" ranking for a highlighted range. `internal/metricalert` evaluates Netdata-style rules (lookup, calc/warn/crit expressions, hysteresis, delays, silences, ack) with 46 built in, notifying through the existing channels and never touching enforcement. App collectors cover nginx, Apache, HAProxy, Redis, memcached, Envoy, CoreDNS, etcd and any Prometheus endpoint, with credentials from environment variables only and optional discovery by comm and socket inode (never cmdline). Exporters: Prometheus remote write, OTLP JSON, Graphite. Netra-only: `GET /api/v1/metrics/evidence` joins a chart window to flows, drop reasons, TCP/DNS/HTTP boards and captures; AI briefs cite metric alerts and anomalies; `GET /api/v1/metrics/fleet` rolls up peer clusters. Surfaces: `/api/v1/metrics/*`, `netractl metrics`, MCP `netra_metrics_*`, console Metrics/Anomalies/Alerts pages. Tests: `scripts/ci-metrics-unit.sh` (job `go`) and `scripts/ci-metrics-veth.sh` (job `metrics-veth-smoke`: real collectors and netrad, iperf3 on a veth, anomaly flagged, alert raised, evidence answered), both passing on a live lab host. Docs: `docs/metrics.md`, `docs/metric-alerts.md`, `docs/anomaly-detection.md`, `docs/app-collectors.md`.
- **Kubernetes list calls page, and pod lists skip terminated pods.** On a lab cluster with 12,629 pods (over 11,000 `Evicted`) the pod list was 72 MB and the client cut every response at 8 MiB, so the insights boards, `ebpf workloads` and `policy simulate` failed with "unexpected end of JSON input". Pods, services and events are now listed with `limit`/`continue`. `ListPods` and `ListWorkloads` also ask the API server for `status.phase!=Failed,status.phase!=Succeeded`: terminated pods cannot own traffic and their stale IPs may belong to a new pod, and on that cluster the list went from 10.7 s per page to 129 pods in under a second. `GET /api/v1/pods` still lists every pod.

- **Node isolation: a per-node allow-only egress filter, in shadow or enforce.** Until now Netra could not say "this node may only talk to these destinations": `allowed_*` maps are exceptions to deny rules, default-deny exists only per workload, and observe mode does not count what enforce would drop. `bpf/netra_nodeiso.c` is a standalone TCX program at the head of each agent interface's egress chain that judges new outbound TCP connections and all UDP against up to 64 CIDR + protocol + port-range rules; shadow counts would-block packets and bytes and the destinations behind them, enforce drops them. It is independent of the cluster mode and of `config_map`. Replies to inbound connections, flows established before the policy, ICMP/ICMPv6, DHCP and non-first fragments always pass, local UDP services can be exempted by source port (SSH's 22 by default), and the agent always allows its controller. Enforce carries a lease (15 m default, at most 1 h) and falls back to shadow when it lapses, when the controller goes stale, when an apply fails, and on a controller restart (shadow policies persist). Policies are replaced atomically by generation. `GET/PUT/DELETE /api/v1/ebpf/node-isolation[/{node}]` (`PUT` admin-only), agent `NETRA_NODE_ISOLATION=auto|off|required` (Helm `agent.nodeIsolation`). Ported from FluxVM's pod-policy engine and XDP shield, including its global per-rule match function: the inlined rule scan was rejected by the Linux 7.0 verifier at 1,000,000 instructions, and the ported version verifies in 5,820. Tests: `BPF_PROG_TEST_RUN` verdicts with FluxVM's prefix cases plus shadow/always-pass/generation-swap cases, and a scratch-veth attach with real UDP, both passing on Linux 7.0 and wired into `scripts/ci-ebpf-tests.sh`; unit tests for the ABI, store (lease expiry, restart fail-open) and API. Docs: `docs/node-isolation.md`. Duvora uses this for its shadow/enforce isolation stages.
- **Node isolation attaches where the agent has no interfaces, and coverage reports it.** The first live deploy (Linux 7.0, k3s with Cilium) loaded `netra_nodeiso` but attached it nowhere: in cgroup mode beside Cilium the agent has no TC interfaces, so the list was empty. It now attaches on `NETRA_NODE_ISOLATION_INTERFACES` (Helm `agent.nodeIsolationInterfaces`) when set, else on the agent's interfaces, else on the interfaces of the IPv4 and IPv6 default routes (read from `/proc/net/route` and `/proc/net/ipv6_route`); on that host that is `eno8303`. `netra_nodeiso_egress` also now appears in program health and `/api/v1/ebpf/coverage`, which previously listed only the main datapath collection. Live test on that host, driven from Duvora: shadow counted SYNs to 1.1.1.1:443 and NTP while traffic flowed; enforce dropped them (and upstream DNS) while SSH, the controller and Duvora stayed reachable; the kill switch and release restored egress.
- **Stable controller certificate.** The chart minted a new self-signed certificate on every controller pod start, so no client could pin it. `tls.existingSecret` serves a `kubernetes.io/tls` Secret instead, and `deploy-remote.sh` creates `netra-system/netra-tls` once (SANs as before plus the host IP), reuses it on later deploys, and saves its certificate to `~/.netra/tls.crt` for clients such as Duvora (`NETRA_TLS_PERSIST=0` keeps the per-pod certificate).

## 0.29.0 — 2026-09-27

- **Dashboard and docs site follow one apple.com UX contract (`docs/design/APPLE-UX-CONTRACT.md`).** The dashboard now defaults to Apple light (white page, flat hairline cards); dark stays one click away, and a stored `netra-theme` choice still wins, so existing users see no change. Buttons collapse to four looks (blue primary, neutral, tinted state, red destructive) with no `!important`, metric tiles become a hairline metric band, the login is a single centered composition, and the Workloads "Per page" row no longer overlaps its neighbouring text. New tokens (`--surface-1`, `--hairline-1`, `--shadow-1/2/3`, `--focus-ring`) and primitives (`styles/apple-story.css`, `components/Toolbar.tsx`) are shared; `website/src/css/custom.css` maps Infima to the same values. A full-page audit of all 28 pages in both themes and at 390px (no page errors, no horizontal overflow, no sub-3:1 text) also fixed: unstyled filter inputs on Connections/Workloads, red tab pills on Surfaces (red is now only for "Remove …" chips), stretched checkboxes, oversized empty-state text, ~2.5:1 inline `code`, and `Policies`/`Topology` crashing on an empty response. Nav data now lives in `web/src/lib/navGroups.ts` with a test that every page is reachable from the menu. A second audit against a populated controller (real API shapes, error states) also fixed: cards clipped off the right edge on phones (grid tracks are now `minmax(0, 1fr)`), the Diagnostics menu running past short viewports, `.card p` overriding purpose-built paragraph classes (login error, snapshot time, Ask Netra meta), bright accent text at ~2:1 on light surfaces (new `--accent-*-text` tokens), stretched severity badges, numbers wrapping mid-digit in metric bands, and raw `{"error":…}` JSON showing in the UI (`api()` now unwraps it). Unlabelled inputs on L7, Policies and Firewall gained accessible names, and the phone nav now hints that it scrolls. Card titles are now `<h2>` (page `h1` → card `h2` → sub-heading `h3`), so no page skips a heading level; verified on all 28 pages against mock and real data. Incidents ranks each cluster's signals by severity, shows the top four, and expands the rest on request (a 15,896px page became about 4,757px, and open clusters stay open across the 20s refresh). The Policies GitOps hint no longer repeats the controller's identical message. Two more fixes surfaced while closing out the audit: closing the connection-evidence dialog now returns keyboard focus to the "Explain connection" button that opened it (it was captured after the Close button's `autoFocus` had already taken focus, so focus fell to the page body; this was also the one browser-smoke assertion that had been failing), and the Ask Netra status line reads "Heuristic engine only · Mutations: never — AI endpoints are read-only" instead of splicing the server's clause after a bare "·" (and no longer prints "undefined" when fields are missing). A first deployment to a fresh K3s host then audited every page against a real agent's data (no page errors, no 5xx, no clipping, no contrast failures); that pass moved the last `h3` card title on Workloads ("Unattributed traffic") to `h2`, raised the compact Explain buttons to 36px, and extended the Congestion "?" glossary touch targets to 36px without changing their look. The `docs/ux` login, Overview, Path and Congestion screenshots were re-captured from that deployment (the login page's host is redacted); the Flows, Firewall and Drops images, the policy/lockdown/capture images and the GIFs still show the previous design. No API or routing contract changed.

## 0.28.5 — 2026-09-22

- **Netlink changes now say who made them.** The recorder hears every link, address, route and neighbor change over RTNL multicast, but a multicast notification does not name its requester. `bpf/netra_rtnl.c` is an `fentry` on `rtnetlink_rcv_msg`, which runs synchronously in the requesting task, so the current task is the requester. For each request that modifies state it records the process's comm, pid, cgroup id, the RTM_* message type, the interface the request names (an index: a fixed field for address and neighbor requests and `RTA_OIF` for a route; or, for a link request that names its device only by name, which is how modern `ip link set dev X` works, the `IFLA_IFNAME` and the message flags) and, for a route, its destination (`RTA_DST` and prefix length) into a ring buffer; read-only requests (RTM_GET*, dumps) are dropped in the kernel before anything is reserved. It captures no argv or environment and nothing of a message beyond the interface (index or name) and a route's destination (values the recorder already publishes about the change), and only observes; it counts what it drops when the ring is full. `internal/rtnlactor` joins each recorded change to the request behind it by message type, interface and time, and every event now carries `origin` and, when a process did it, `actor` (comm, pid, cgroup, and the pod when the requester is in one). The join is deliberately strict about what it claims: exactly one requester is `probable`; several at that moment are `ambiguous` and listed, never picked between; and `origin: kernel` (nobody requested it: carrier loss, kernel timers, router advertisements) is claimed only while the sensor was already running, the kernel has not dropped requests around then and the ring still reaches back far enough, otherwise the event is simply left unattributed. Interface matching is what stops a veth's carrier loss being credited to the `ip link set <peer> down` issued at the same instant, and for routes the destination prefix separates two processes adding routes on one interface at the same moment (all found on real kernels: the first version reported two same-interface routes as ambiguous, correctly, and matching on the destination resolves it; a link request that names its device only by `IFLA_IFNAME` now explains only that device, with a create exempt because it also creates a peer; and it credited a veth's carrier loss to `ip link set <peer> down` because every `ip link` command first sends a bare 32-byte `RTM_NEWLINK` probe with no index, name or attributes, which a join read as "names no interface" and matched to everything, so the kernel program now records the message length, that probe explains nothing, and a request with attributes but no readable name is uncertain: it names nobody and does not let the change be called the kernel's). Findings name the requester in their message (`default route (via 10.0.0.1 on eth0) was removed from the main table by calico-node (pid 42, pod kube-system/calico-node-x)`, or `... went down: eth1 (down) by the kernel (no process requested it)`, which separates an administrator's `ip link set down` from a pulled cable) and say nothing when the sensor could not tell; a finding built from events with different requesters is not summarised as one. Events are attributed on the copies a report carries, held back about 300 ms so their request record has arrived, and the ring stays raw. `NETRA_RTNL_ACTOR=auto|off` (Helm `agent.rtnlActor`); it needs kernel BTF and `fentry`, and where the kernel cannot run it the report says `unavailable` with the reason and the recorder carries on unattributed. Tests: `TestRTNLActor` on a real kernel loads the object and changes the network from the test process and from a child `ip`, requires each record to name its requester, a read-only dump to record nothing and nothing to be dropped; and runs the real recorder, sensor and joiner together, requiring the route `ip` added to be attributed to `ip`, this process's route to this process, an admin link-down to `ip`, and the veth's carrier loss to the kernel and not to that `ip`. Unit tests cover the decoder, every window edge, interface mismatch, ambiguity and each situation where `kernel` must not be claimed (`scripts/ci-netlink-unit.sh`). Limits: the join is by message type, interface, destination and time, so it is a probable match and not proof; two requests for the very same prefix, or two requesters for one non-route object in the same instant, are reported as ambiguous; a multipath route names no single interface and matches any; a link request that names its device by an alternative name is read as nobody's (kernel); the program records only the agent's own network namespace (interface indexes are per namespace, so a pod's request for its `ifindex 3` must not be credited with a host change on the host's `ifindex 3`): it reads the requester's namespace with three CO-RE member reads, the agent names the one to record through a one-element map, requests from any other namespace are dropped in the kernel before anything is reserved (which also keeps pod churn out of the ring buffer), and the joiner refuses to match across namespaces as a second guard. An earlier version of these notes claimed only host-namespace matches were used before that was true; this is the change that makes it so.
- **`deploy-remote.sh` can no longer leave the controller down while helm reports success.** Deploying 0.28.4 to the live host took the controller down for about ten minutes. The script builds the images on the host and imports them into k3s's containerd under a fixed tag (`ghcr.io/zyvorai/netra:0.27.81`), then runs `helm upgrade`. Kubelet garbage-collects images no pod uses once the disk passes 85%, and the host was at 84%: the freshly imported controller image was collected before its new pod started, the pod stuck in `ImagePullBackOff` ("NotFound": the fixed tag is on no registry), `helm --wait` still passed, and the script died at a 180 s `rollout status` timeout, so the agent restart was skipped too. Rolling back would not have helped, because the previous revision uses the same tag. The fix is in `scripts/lib/deploy-guards.sh`, sourced on the host: the deploy warns when the disk is at 80% or more and refuses at 95% (`NETRA_DEPLOY_WARN_DISK_PCT`, `NETRA_DEPLOY_MAX_DISK_PCT`, `NETRA_DEPLOY_SKIP_DISK_CHECK=1`); builds both images first and imports them only right before helm, so the GC window is seconds instead of the whole agent build; ensures each image is present before `helm upgrade` and before each restart, re-importing the local build if it is missing; and waits for the pods to be Ready (`NETRA_DEPLOY_READY_TIMEOUT`, default 600 s), detecting `ImagePullBackOff`/`ErrImagePull`, repairing it (re-import, delete only the stuck pod so it restarts at once) and failing loudly with the pods printed if they never come up. The first live use of the guards found a third, which the offline test could not: the wait was "every matching pod is Ready", but right after `rollout restart` the old pod is still Ready and its replacement does not exist yet, so it returned at once and the deploy reported "NETRA ready" while the new pods were still starting. It now waits for `kubectl rollout status` (polled in short slices so a stuck pull can be repaired between them), with a regression test that keeps a Ready old pod in view while the rollout is incomplete and requires the wait to keep polling; mutation-checked. Two bugs in the first version of the guards were caught by the new offline test and fixed before it shipped: under `set -o pipefail` an unreadable `df` would have aborted the deploy despite being meant as a warning, and `ctr images ls | grep -q` can SIGPIPE `ctr` on a long image list and report a present image as missing. `scripts/ci-deploy-guards.sh` (job `go`) runs the guards against stubbed `df`, `k3s`, `podman` and `kubectl`, reproduces the outage (image GC'd, pod stuck, repaired), and renders the real remote script to check it parses and orders builds, imports, ensure and helm correctly; it is mutation-checked. Not covered: this cannot make a full disk healthy, and a controller image that is genuinely gone from the host's build cache still needs a rebuild.


## 0.28.4 — 2026-09-21

- **BPF attachment inventory: Netra can now tell when its own hooks have silently gone.** Netra resolves its interface list once at agent start and attaches with `bpf_link`, so a NIC or bond that is deleted and recreated under the same name loses its hooks (a link dies with its device) while the agent's own hook list still says they are attached; the traffic on that interface is then no longer observed and nothing said so. `internal/bpfattach` asks the kernel what is attached, read-only: XDP from the interface's netlink attributes, TCX in execution order from the kernel's program query, and classic `cls_bpf` filters from the netlink filter list, keeping only each program's id, kernel name and an owner class (`netra`, `cilium`, `other`). It never reads bytecode or a map and never attaches, detaches or replaces anything, so the Cilium coexistence rules are unchanged. `internal/bpfattachdiag` compares it with the agent's hook list: `bpf-netra-hook-missing` and `bpf-xdp-replaced` (warning), `bpf-no-interface-coverage` (info, for an agent with `agent.interfaces` unset, which sees cgroup-level traffic only) and `bpf-inventory-unavailable`. It never guesses: no finding when the kernel cannot list TCX (before 6.6), when the query for that interface failed, when the list was capped, or when the controller does not hold the list the summary refers to, and it accepts the kernel's program name in either form, full or truncated to 15 characters (`netra_edge_ingress` or `netra_edge_ingr`): a real-kernel test showed current x86_64 and arm64 kernels return the full name, and matching only the truncated form would have reported every long-named Netra program (XDP, edge, capture) as missing. Findings are level-triggered and flow through `internal/health`, so they reach the alert poller, incidents, the AI brief and the SIEM export. The kernel is read every 30 s and the list is sent only when it changed or every 5 minutes (a hash-checked summary otherwise, restored by the controller). `NETRA_BPF_ATTACH=auto|off` (Helm `agent.bpfAttach`), `GET /api/v1/ebpf/attachments`, `netractl ebpf attachments`, `netra_bpf_attach_*` gauges (the only label is the finding severity). It reports drift and does not repair it: the fix is still an agent restart. Tests: `scripts/ci-bpfattach-unit.sh` (job `go`) and, on a real kernel, `scripts/ci-ebpf-tests.sh` attaches Netra's real programs to a scratch veth beside another program (TCX, XDP, a classic `cls_bpf` filter), detaches a hook, and deletes and recreates the interface; that step has not run in CI yet, so treat the kernel behaviour as unverified until the `ebpf` job is green. Docs: `docs/bpf-attachments.md`.

## 0.28.3 — 2026-09-21

- **Netlink findings: the recorder now says what is wrong, and alerts on it.** `internal/netlinkdiag` derives evidence-backed findings from the recorded changes: default route removed (critical for IPv4), default gateway unreachable (its neighbor entry is `FAILED`, critical), other failed neighbors, a host uplink down or deleted (critical if it carried the default route), an uplink's MTU changed, and the recorder's own overruns. They are level-triggered, so a finding clears when the latest snapshot shows the network recovered, and a fresh neighbor or link change waits up to 45 s for a snapshot to confirm it. Noise is designed out: only host uplinks (`device`, `bond`, `team`, `vlan`) can go "down" (pod veths, tunnels and bridges such as libvirt's `virbr0` are normal churn), `INCOMPLETE` is ARP in flight and never a failure, one failed neighbor is info and three or more a warning, and the subject is always the node. They flow through `internal/health`, so they reach the alert poller (source `health`, so existing channels and severity filters apply), incidents, the AI brief and the SIEM export with no extra wiring, and two on one node correlate; they do not move the health score. `GET /api/v1/netlink/findings` (`window`, `node`; `evaluated` and `skipped` so silence cannot pass for health), `netractl netlink findings`, and `netra_netlink_findings{severity}`. Messages carry interface names and IPs, never MAC addresses; the evidence, which does, stays in the authenticated API. Tests cover every detector and its false-positive case, including that `lower-layer-down` is recognised (the first design matched a string the library never emits), the health integration, and the alert poller end to end (one critical notification, de-duplicated, and none once the route returns). Nothing here says why a change happened.

## 0.28.2 — 2026-09-21

- **Netlink change recorder: which link, address, route or neighbor changed on a node, and when.** A read-only RTNL sensor in the agent (`internal/netlinkwatch`, `NETRA_NETLINK=auto|off`, Helm `agent.netlink`, ring size `agent.netlinkEventBuffer`) records link, address, route (every routing table, not just main) and neighbor changes plus a full snapshot every 30 s and after any loss. `GET /api/v1/netlink` (`view=events|state|all`, `node`, `kind`, `since`, `limit`), `netractl netlink state|events`, and fixed-cardinality `netra_netlink_*` gauges (the only label is the five-value event kind). It never changes a link, address, route, rule, neighbour or qdisc, loads no eBPF program, and touches no Cilium object. Built from the external `Netra-Netlink-Recorder-v1` design, which could not ship as written and was reworked rather than applied (`git apply` failed on every modified file, and the new code had these faults): it was never wired into the agent or the report, so it recorded nothing; the netlink library ends a subscription on the first receive error (`ENOBUFS` in a route storm) and never reopens it, so one storm blinded it until restart, and kernel-side loss was not counted; the link-down check compared against `lowerlayerdown` while the library says `lower-layer-down`, and would have flagged every new veth as critical; the cursor advanced on read so a failed POST lost events; the full snapshot rode every 3 s report; and addresses, routes and neighbors carried an ifindex but no interface name. The recorder now resubscribes (immediately after an overflow, with capped backoff for other faults) and records an `overrun` event; delivery is committed only after the controller accepts the report and the controller dedupes by `(epoch, cursor)`; the snapshot ships only when it changed (or every 5 min); neighbor reachable/stale/probe churn is counted (`suppressed`) instead of evicting real changes; and `dropped`, `missed`, `overruns` and `resubscribes` separate a quiet node from a lossy timeline. Limits, stated in `docs/netlink-recorder.md`: host network namespace only, history is controller memory (cleared on restart, `historySince` marks the blind spot), and there are no findings, alerts or drop correlation yet. Tests: `scripts/ci-netlink-unit.sh` (job `go`) and `scripts/ci-netlink-veth.sh` (job `netlink-veth-smoke`: a real veth in a throwaway namespace, plus a forced overrun). The real-kernel job has not run yet; treat the kernel behaviour as unverified until it is green.
- **`kind-lifecycle` no longer fails when the repository has no release tag.** It skips with a warning annotation (CI sets `ALLOW_NO_PREVIOUS_TAG=1`); a local run stays strict. The install, upgrade and rollback test does not run until a tag exists.
- **Verified: 0.28.1 installs and runs on a real node, and its UI is complete.** The two released images were pulled from `ghcr.io` onto the K3s lab host with the chart from the `v0.28.1` tag (`--reset-then-reuse-values`); both running pods reported the exact release digests, the controller and agent reported 0.28.1, the cookie session, 12 read endpoints and `/metrics` passed, and `scripts/check-served-ui.sh` confirmed the logo and favicon are served and identical to the repository files. The install script's own digest and agent checks were fixed first: the previous run had silently skipped its digest check.
- **Docs quote the 0.28.1 digests and signing identity** (`deploy/README.md`, the website quickstart) and say to use 0.28.1 or later.

## 0.28.1 — 2026-09-20

- **Fix: the 0.28.0 images shipped without the logo and the favicon.** The Dockerfile's web stage copied `web/src` but not `web/public`, where `zyvor-logomark.svg` and `zyvor-mark.svg` live, so every image built from it served the app's HTML fallback (HTTP 200) for those two paths: the nav logo was a broken image and the browser tab had no icon. The health probes, the release dry run and the lifecycle test all passed, because an unknown path answers 200. `web/public` is now copied, and `scripts/check-served-ui.sh` fetches every file in `web/public` and every asset the served page references from a running controller, failing on a non-200, on the HTML fallback, or on any byte that differs from the repository file. It runs in the release dry run against both the controller image and the thin runtime image, and in `kind-lifecycle` against the upgraded controller. Use 0.28.1; the 0.28.0 images have the defect (the controller itself is unaffected).
- **Verified: the released 0.28.0 installs and runs on a real node.** On the K3s lab host the two released images were pulled from `ghcr.io` (not built from source), installed with the chart from the `v0.28.0` tag and `--reset-then-reuse-values`, and both running pods reported the exact release digests as their `imageID`. The controller reported 0.28.0, the agent reported within seconds (observe mode, 16 hooks), the cookie session had `HttpOnly`, `Secure` and `SameSite=Strict`, the cookie held no API key, sign-out ended the session, and 12 read endpoints answered 200. The keyless signing certificate names `release.yml@refs/tags/v0.28.0` and the tagged commit.
- **Docs: how to cut and install a release.** `docs/ci.md` has a "Cutting a release" section (bump, gate, dry run, tag, verify the registry, install the released images). `deploy/README.md` has "Install from a release": the release digests, the exact cosign identity, and the Helm command that keeps an install's values. The dashboard login doc lists the session cookie's attributes, its 12-hour lifetime, that rotating the API key ends every session, and the one-time migration of an old `netra-token`. `docs/social/README.md` lists the social assets and how each is rebuilt.
- **Fix: `kind-lifecycle` no longer upgrades a release to itself.** It picked the newest tag whose commit was not `HEAD`, so any commit on top of a fresh tag (still the tag's version) selected that tag as the "previous" release and tripped the "nothing is being upgraded" assertion. It now picks the newest tag with a different version.

## 0.28.0 — 2026-09-20

- **First tagged release since 0.27.97, and the first published to ghcr.io.** The release workflow was added after `v0.27.97` was tagged, so no earlier version has a published image. Versions 0.27.98 to 0.27.111 were built and deployed but never tagged, so this release carries all of them (their entries are below) together with everything under this heading. The minor version moves because dashboard sign-in changed: the browser now holds an HttpOnly session cookie and the bearer token is no longer kept in local or session storage (a leftover `netra-token` from an older UI is migrated to a session on first load and removed).
- **Licence: no Apache-2.0 wording remains.** The Enterprise Pricing sheets (the five JPEGs, the PDF, and the website copy) described Community as Apache-2.0. They now say what `LICENSE` says: the Zyvor Production License v1.0 is free for evaluation and non-production use, and production use needs a commercial license (Community is shown as free for non-production; the tagline "Open dataplane" became "Source-available dataplane"). The sheets are rebuilt from a new source in the repository, `docs/sales/enterprise-pricing/sheets.html` with `build.sh`, because the old images and PDF had none. The README and website share card (`docs/social/netra-share-card.png`) carried an "APACHE 2.0" pill and "v0.14" in its pixels; it now shows "ZYVOR PRODUCTION LICENSE" and v0.28. Apache-2.0 now appears only in `go.mod`, as the licence of third-party dependencies.

- **CI now runs every use case for real.** New jobs, each a script that fails on the first bad assertion and was mutation-checked on real runners: `enforce-veth` (a real agent drops exactly what deny/CIDR/port rules name in enforce mode and never in observe, ingress vs egress, IPv6, lease expiry, fail-open when the controller dies), `capture-live` (both capture backends to a `.pcap` that tcpdump reads), `mcp-live`, `persistence-live` (state survives `kill -9`; an enforcement lease is never resurrected; a corrupt state file is refused and left untouched), `sinks-live` (webhook, Slack, Teams, HTTP bridge, SMTP, OTLP and syslog to loopback receivers, with signatures, retry, severity filter and no secret leaked), `doctor-live`, `kind-lifecycle` (previous release -> `helm upgrade --reset-then-reuse-values` with the agent DaemonSet -> restart -> `helm rollback`), `manifests-kind` (the plain `kubectl apply -k deploy/` path) and `web-e2e` (a real browser against a real controller). `ci-netractl-live.sh` now also runs the mutating commands (146 pass, was 122 with 25 skipped), `hack/smoke.sh` and `examples/siem-export.sh`. `docs/ci.md` maps every use case to its job.
- **Hygiene gates.** gofmt, shellcheck and `go.mod` tidiness are enforced; `golangci-lint` reports only issues a change introduces; a pinned `govulncheck` fails on a reachable known vulnerability; CodeQL and dependabot are on. To get there: 17 files were formatted, six shellcheck warnings cleared, and grpc (1.83.1), x/text (0.39.0) and the AWS S3 client (1.97.3) bumped for three vulnerabilities the code could reach.
- **Nightly and release dry-run.** `nightly.yml` runs the whole suite under `-race`, 10 minutes of fuzzing, the privileged suites on `ubuntu-24.04`, `ubuntu-24.04-arm` and `ubuntu-22.04` (a 6.8 azure kernel; clang 18 is installed there because the image's clang 14 cannot build the objects), multi-arch image builds and two-replica HA, and keeps one "Nightly CI is failing" issue. `release-dryrun.yml` builds both images and boots the controller and the thin runtime image on pull requests that could break a release.
- **Fix: one failing feed no longer blanks the Firewall page or zeroes the Overview.** Found by the new web e2e against a controller with no Kubernetes API. Both pages loaded their feeds with `Promise.all`, so a single 502 (`/api/v1/ebpf/workloads`, `/api/v1/insights/summary`) discarded every result: the Firewall page showed no rules and never displayed a rule added through it, and every Overview board read 0 while the brief beside them said "1 agents". They now use `Promise.allSettled`: each feed applies on its own and the first failure is still shown.
- **Fix: typing in an investigation filter jumped back to the Overview, and phones scrolled sideways.** A Nav click showed the page but left the URL hash empty, while the Connections and Workloads filters and "Copy link" read the page from the hash, so with an empty hash typing in "namespace (exact)" navigated to the Overview and a copied link pointed there. Nav clicks now keep the hash in step (`replaceState`: no history entry, filters carried over). Separately, the decorative hero glow reached 8% past its box, so the Overview scrolled sideways at every width (8 px at 390 px, 57 px at 1440 px); `main` now clips horizontal overflow (`overflow-x: clip`, no scroll container, so sticky elements are unaffected). Both were found by `web/tests/investigation-smoke.cjs`, which had gone stale (it waited for an Overview heading a redesign removed, and it runs only on pull requests that touch `web/`); it is updated to the current UI and was mutation-checked against both fixes (including a desktop-width assertion).
- **Fix: two CI flakes.** `TestL7SampleIPv6` returned as soon as the first sample arrived and asserted a served request, so on a slower kernel the egress sample could beat the ingress one; it now waits for the serving side. `TestDumpIsCorrectWhileOtherListenersChurn` assumed `inet_diag` dumps the listener hash atomically, which it does not (a stable listener can be skipped by one dump under extreme churn); it now asserts the listener is never missing persistently and that misses stay rare, and `docs/listen-queues.md` records the limit.
- **Product brochure rebuilt** (`docs/sales/brochure/`, 17 pages, regenerated with `build.py`): how eBPF does it, the eight eBPF objects, a four-part pictorial walkthrough of a traffic-drop incident (detection, capturing the node's packets, triage, leased containment), the newest kernel sensors with measured costs, an honest privacy table, access and export, and how it is tested. It corrects claims that had gone stale: the license (Zyvor Production License v1.0, not Apache-2.0), "no application payloads" (none by default; capture and sampling are opt-in and limited), and the MCP tool count (180, not 133). The DOCX copies were removed because they still carried the old claims. The README license badge and the MCP doc's tool counts were corrected too. The remaining Apache-2.0 / "open source" wording was then fixed too: `python/pyproject.toml`, the field sheet (HTML and PDF), the Product Perspective deck (PPTX and PDF, regenerated with LibreOffice; only the two affected labels changed), the buyers guide, `docs/packetwolf.md` and `docs/dashboard-login.md`. Not changed: the Enterprise Pricing sheets, whose packaging is built on an Apache-2.0 "Community" edition and needs a business decision, not a search and replace.
- **Docs.** README: the MCP tool counts were stale (180 tools: 120 read, 60 mutating), the docs index was missing 38 documents, the repository tree missed the newer packages and objects, and the recent sensors, sinks and access controls are listed. Each feature doc now says which CI job proves it, and `docs/otlp-push.md` and `docs/l7-metadata.md` were corrected to what CI now shows.

## 0.27.111 — 2026-09-19

- **Mutual TLS between agents and the controller (opt-in, off by default).** `NETRA_AGENT_MTLS=optional|required` with `NETRA_AGENT_CLIENT_CA` makes netrad verify a client certificate from the agent; the agent presents `NETRA_CLIENT_CERT`/`NETRA_CLIENT_KEY` (and can trust a private CA with `NETRA_CA_FILE`). Nothing changes until a mode is set: the chart renders byte-identical output by default.
- **It is a second factor, not a replacement.** In `required` mode an agent-only request (report, capture stream, and the agent-key path of `/api/v1/ebpf/config`) needs the agent key **and** a certificate signed by the agent CA with the client-auth key usage. A leaked agent key alone no longer works. People are never asked for a certificate: the browser, `netractl`, API keys, probes and `/metrics` are untouched, because the listener asks for a certificate and verifies one only if it is offered.
- **Fails closed, everywhere it can.** netrad refuses to start on a typo'd mode, a missing or empty CA file, or `required` over plain HTTP (a client certificate cannot be requested there, and pretending otherwise would leave an operator believing agents are authenticated). A certificate signed by another CA, expired, or without the client-auth usage fails the handshake. An agent given an unusable certificate refuses to run instead of reporting into a wall. A bad mode that reaches the server anyway means `required`, not `off`.
- **A staged rollout.** `optional` verifies certificates when offered and records it: `/api/v1/agents` items gain `"mtls": true` (set by the controller from the handshake, never taken from the agent's report) and `/metrics` gains `netra_agent_mtls_reports_total` and `netra_agent_mtls_rejected_total`, so which agents have moved over is visible before `required` is switched on.
- **Renewal without a restart.** The agent re-reads its certificate and key when the files change (cert-manager rewrites them in place), keeps using the previous pair while the two files briefly disagree mid-rotation, and applies the same certificate to the packet-capture websocket.
- **Chart.** `mtls.mode`, `mtls.secretName` (a Secret with `ca.crt`, `tls.crt`, `tls.key`) or `mtls.certManager` (a Certificate for a CA issuer, ECDSA P-256, renewed automatically). The controller mounts only `ca.crt`, never the agents' key; template-time errors for a bad mode, `tls.enabled=false`, or no certificate source.
- **What it is not.** One certificate is shared by all agents, so it proves "an agent", not which node: a compromised agent can still name another node in its report, exactly as with the shared agent key. Per-node certificates are a larger change. The controller reads its CA at startup; rotating the CA needs a controller restart. See `docs/agent-mtls.md`.
- **Tested against real TLS, with mutation checks.** Real listeners and real certificates (handshake success and failure by issuer, key usage, expiry; optional vs required; rotation and half-rotation), API tests over a real TLS listener, agent tests for the HTTP client and the capture websocket, and `scripts/ci-mtls-smoke.sh`, which runs the real netrad (all five unsafe settings refused; certificate, issuer, usage and key enforced) and, on Linux, the real agent with a certificate, without one, and with the certificate replaced under it. Six enforcement points and both agent wiring points were mutated and each mutation is caught by a test.

## 0.27.110 — 2026-09-19

- **TLS plaintext sampling: HTTPS operation counts, read where the application hands bytes to OpenSSL.** Uprobes on `SSL_write`, `SSL_read` and their `_ex` variants (`bpf/netra_ssl.c`) give HTTPS the same bounded counts the packet-level sampler gives cleartext: HTTP method, status and host, and HTTP/2 / gRPC operation names, per role (served / issued). `GET /api/v1/l7/tls` and the `netra_tls_sample_*` metric family. **Off by default and opt-in**: `NETRA_TLS_UPROBES=auto|required` (Helm `agent.tlsUprobes`, `tlsUprobesComms`, `tlsUprobesGap`). New: `internal/sslprobe`, `docs/tls-plaintext.md`.
- **This reverses a documented non-goal, deliberately, with limits.** The docs said "no decrypt". That still holds by default and for everything else; this is the one explicit, opt-in exception, and the docs (`competitive-observability.md`, `p5-surfaces.md`, `l7-sampling.md`) are updated so they no longer contradict it. It is not TLS interception: no keys, certificates or proxy; the application decrypts its own traffic and the probe copies the first 128 bytes it was handed or returned. Those bytes go kernel → agent memory → parser → discarded, never to disk, a log, or the controller. What leaves the agent is an allowlisted operation name and a coarse outcome: never a path, query string, header, cookie, bearer token or body.
- **Limit what it can see.** `NETRA_TLS_UPROBES_COMMS=nginx,envoy` restricts it to named processes, **enforced in the kernel before any byte is copied**; a real-kernel test proves a process off the list produces no event, and a mutant that removes the check is caught. Enabling it sets `hostPID` on the agent DaemonSet (to find other workloads' libraries), which the chart renders only when this or process metadata is on; CI asserts a default render has neither.
- **Tested with secrets, on real kernels.** Real HTTPS between real processes on the GitHub runners with secrets planted in the URL **path, query string, a cookie and a bearer token**: none appears in the API, the agent report, `/metrics` or either process's logs, and per-role counts are exact. Verified on **Linux 6.17 x86_64 and arm64** (OpenSSL 3.0.13), where one object serves both architectures because the register offsets are written at load time from the running architecture.
- **Two counting defects found by reading the real output, not by an assertion.** The first end-to-end run passed but reported `hosts: localhost GET 30` for 15 requests (the host table merged both roles, so every request counted twice) and `eligible 89 / emitted 87` with the rate limit off (calls skipped by the allowlist were counted as candidates, inflating the scale factor). Host counts are now per role, and `eligible` counts only observed-process calls so `eligible = emitted + rateLimited + lost` and the scale factor is exactly 1 when nothing is limited. This also corrects the packet-level sampler's host table, which had the same double count.
- **The whole privileged eBPF suite now runs on real arm64 kernels in CI** (`ebpf-arm64`), which it never had: the agent image ships for arm64, and the CO-RE drop attribution, the TCP event layouts, listen queues, the packet-level L7 sampler and this feature's register layout were all only ever exercised on x86_64. All of it passes.
- **Cost, measured** (kernel accounting on real runners): about 1.0 µs of program time per probe on x86_64 and 1.4 µs on arm64; about 3 to 4% on an HTTPS request that includes a full handshake, trap included (`TestSSLCostPerCall` re-measures it in CI). Long-lived connections making many small calls pay per call; the rate limit limits samples, not probe entries. Documented with the other limits: dynamically linked OpenSSL only (not static builds, Go `crypto/tls`, BoringSSL, GnuTLS, NSS or Java/Node bundles).
- **CI.** `scripts/ci-sslprobe-unit.sh` in the `go` job; the privileged script's new TLS step (fails on a skip) runs in both `ebpf` and `ebpf-arm64`; a new `tlssample-smoke` job runs the real agent and controller end to end; helm asserts TLS sampling renders off and `hostPID` absent by default. Mutation-checked: inverting the role inference, attaching deleted libraries, leaking the request target into the host field, and removing the in-kernel allowlist each fail a test.

## 0.27.109 — 2026-09-19

- **Sampled L7 protocol observation: which Redis commands, SQL verbs, Kafka APIs and gRPC methods workloads use, and how often they fail.** A new standalone `cgroup_skb` program (`bpf/netra_l7sample.c`) copies up to 128 payload bytes of TCP segments to or from a configured service port into a ring buffer, rate-limited per flow and direction; the agent classifies them (`internal/l7sample`) into bounded operation names and discards the bytes. `GET /api/v1/l7/sampled` and `netra_l7_sample_*` metrics. **Off by default** because it reads application payloads: `NETRA_L7_SAMPLE=auto|required` (Helm `agent.l7Sample`, `l7SamplePorts`, `l7SampleGap`).
- **Privacy is enforced by the output, and tested.** Every field a parser can return is an allowlisted operation name, a coarse outcome or a validated pattern (Redis command, SQL verb, SQLSTATE class, Kafka API, gRPC `/pkg.Svc/Method`, HTTP status): keys, SQL text, topics, paths, cookies and bearer tokens are read only to classify and never appear in a result. Tests plant secrets in every protocol and require they never surface; 200 000 random/mutated inputs and a 20 s CI fuzz require every label be bounded and nothing panic; the end-to-end smoke asserts a planted secret appears nowhere the stack exposes (API, agent report, `/metrics`, logs). Cardinality is bounded by construction, with caps as a backstop.
- **Two roles so nothing is counted twice.** A request is seen leaving its client and arriving at its server, so counts carry `role=served` (this node's services) or `role=issued` (its workloads) and must be summed per role, never together. The first end-to-end run showed `GET` at 60 for 30 sent, which is what prompted this.
- **Sampling is honest.** The kernel counts eligible, emitted, rate-limited and lost segments (they add up); `scaleFactor = eligible/emitted` turns sampled counts into estimates, scaled per node. HTTP/2 header compression is stateful, so a header block that references state the sample lacks is reported as `undecodable`, not guessed; Kafka responses and MySQL login packets are not classified. `docs/l7-sampling.md` lists the limits.
- **The real verifier caught a real bug.** The first version was rejected (`R3 unbounded memory access`): after a helper call clang recomputed a copy offset from an unbounded value and deleted the guard it could prove redundant from the source. The bounds checks are now compiler-opaque, and short payloads are copied in fixed chunks with an overlapping tail so **every length from 1 to 140 bytes is captured exactly**, byte for byte, on a real kernel.
- **Cost, measured:** about 29 ns per program run for traffic to a port that is not configured (the common case) and about 65 ns on a configured port with the default rate limit (Linux 6.8 aarch64 VM), per packet in the attached cgroup; re-measured in CI.
- **CI.** `scripts/ci-l7sample-unit.sh` (unit, race, fuzz, minimum test count) in the `go` job; the privileged job runs the kernel tests against the real verifier and real TCP and fails on any skip; a new `l7sample-smoke` job runs the real agent and controller end to end with exact per-role counts; the object ships in the agent image (checked by the image gate); helm asserts sampling renders **off** by default. Mutation-checked: a compiler-visible break of the chunk copy fails the exactness test, an unbounded label fails the label tests, and a secret flowing into an output field fails the leak test.

## 0.27.108 — 2026-09-19

- **The agent reads its big BPF maps about 15 times faster and allocates about 150 times less.** On a live node the agent averaged 194% of a core and 313 MiB. Tracing its `bpf()` calls showed the cause was not the newer sensors but its original flow collection: every 3 seconds it walked every entry of five 131 072-entry LRU hash maps (two syscalls per entry), formatted two IP strings for every entry, sorted them all, and kept 1 000. It now reads in batches (`BPF_MAP_LOOKUP_BATCH`, about a thousand entries per syscall, falling back to iteration on kernels without it) and ranks on raw counters, decoding only the top N (`internal/mapscan`). On 100 000 entries in each of two flow maps `readStats` goes from 275 ms and 253 MB per call to 18.7 ms and 1.6 MB; 60 000 entries take 59 syscalls instead of 120 000. It also stops counting `conntrack` entry by entry.
- **The report is byte-for-byte the same.** The original loops are kept verbatim as a reference (`legacy_readers_test.go`); tests fill real BPF maps of the production shapes, overflowing the 1 000 cap several times, and require the new readers to be deep-equal for `readStats`, `readTCPHealth`, `readUDPFlowHealth` and `readQUICObserved`, plus ties, a missing map, and a map with the wrong layout. They run against a real kernel in the privileged job, which fails if any skip. The one visible difference is an improvement: rows with equal scores now come back in a stable order (the old sort was unstable there).
- **An agent's collection cost is now visible.** Each report carries `mapScans` (entries, syscalls, batched or not, milliseconds per map); the controller exports `netra_agent_map_scan_millis_max`, `netra_agent_map_scan_entries_max` and `netra_agent_map_scan_unbatched_nodes` per map; a read over a second is logged. The problem above was found only by attaching `strace` to a production process. Design and limits: `docs/agent-map-reads.md`.
- **Mutation-checked:** breaking the batch `ENOSPC` growth path, the top-N tie-break, or copying rows out of the reused scan buffer each fails a test.

## 0.27.107 — 2026-09-19

- **Fix: the agent hung forever on its first report on any quiet host.** Each report reads the kernel log (`/dev/kmsg`). It opened the device with `os.OpenFile(…, O_NONBLOCK)`, and in Go a pollable file opened that way does not return `EAGAIN` at the end of the data: the goroutine parks in the runtime poller until more arrives. On a quiet host that is never, so the agent's main loop blocked in its first `syncAndReport` — it never reported, and it could not be stopped by SIGTERM either (the main goroutine was the one stuck). It only escaped when the kernel ring buffer held more than the 256 KiB read cap, which a busy, long-running host does and a fresh node, a kind/dev cluster, a small VM or a CI runner does not; that is why the live cluster's agent was fine. The read now uses raw non-blocking `read(2)`, so `EAGAIN` ends the snapshot as it should, tolerates `/dev/kmsg`'s `EPIPE` (an overwritten record), and uses the 8 KiB buffer the kernel requires for a whole record. Found because `tlsfp-smoke` hung a CI job for six hours: its cleanup `wait`ed on an agent that would not die, and `/api/v1/agents` was empty because it had never reported. Reproduced in a VM (goroutine dump: main goroutine in `kmsg.Snapshot`), then verified on Linux: the old code hangs on both a FIFO and the real `/dev/kmsg`, the new code returns immediately (57 KB of the real log). `tlsfp-smoke`, `http-status-smoke`, `flow-observe-veth` and `auto-capture-veth` all pass in the VM with it.
- **Drop attribution cost, measured.** About 100 ns per dropped packet by the kernel's own BPF accounting on a 4-vCPU aarch64 VM (Linux 6.8) and about 540 ns on a shared GitHub runner (Linux 6.17, x86_64); 150 to 700 ns added to a send-and-drop loop, the worst case; roughly 1 to 5% of a core at 100k drops per second. On a real node it is about 7% of the agent's map syscalls (traced live); the agent's own older flow maps are the rest. `TestDropInfoCostPerDrop` re-measures it on every CI run and fails above 50 µs per drop. See `docs/drop-info.md` for the caveats (one VM, loopback).
- **CI can no longer burn hours.** Every job now has a `timeout-minutes` (it was the 360-minute default), the three veth smoke scripts reap their background processes with a bounded wait and SIGKILL instead of `wait`ing on something that ignores SIGTERM, and the privileged job runs the real-`/dev/kmsg` regression case as root.

## 0.27.106 — 2026-09-19

- **TCP listen-queue pressure: which listener is about to refuse connections.** The kernel already counted listen-queue overflows, but not *whose*. The agent now samples, per TCP listener, the accept-queue depth and the configured backlog, plus the half-open (`SYN_RECV`) connections waiting on it, and keeps each listener's peak and a fill-level histogram. `GET /api/v1/listen-queues?top=&node=` (viewer) and `netra_listen_queue_*` metrics (bounded: six fixed histogram bins, no ports as labels). It reads `inet_diag`, the interface behind `ss -ltn`: **no BPF, no BTF, no root**, so it works on every kernel, and the entire feature is testable against a real kernel without privilege. "Full" is `queue > backlog` as the kernel defines it, so a backlog of *N* holds *N+1*; verified on a real kernel with a backlog-1 listener that never accepts (queue 2, max 1, overflows counted). `NETRA_LISTEN_QUEUES=auto|off`, Helm `agent.listenQueues`. Deliberately not BPF: the plan called for a kernel histogram, but the data is already exposed per listener, and sampling gives the same answer with none of the verifier, BTF or kernel-version risk. The limit is that it samples: a burst that fills and drains between two samples is not seen (the overflow counters still count it).
- **Fix: TCP event sensors now work on newer kernels.** The first GitHub Actions run (Linux 6.17, x86_64) showed `tcp_send_reset` has a different record from 6.8: `sport`/`dport`/`family`/`saddr`/`daddr` are gone, replaced by two 28-byte `sockaddr_in6`-sized blobs. The loader used to refuse it, so the send-reset sensor silently did not run there. It now recognises the blob shape (only when `saddr` is exactly 28 bytes and there is no `sport`, never guessing), reads family, big-endian port and address from it, and still refuses anything else with the reason. The layout tests use the real 6.17 format files, and the `ebpf` job now prints the runner's kernel and tracepoint formats so the next layout change is a labelled failure, not a mystery.
- **Drop-reason numbers differ between kernels, and the names are already right.** `NETFILTER_DROP` is 8 on 6.8 and 12 on 6.17 (`TCP_RESET` 35 vs 45, `UNHANDLED_PROTO` 56 vs 74; 94 vs 124 reasons). Names are taken from the running kernel's own table, so drop attribution was correct on both; a test now pins both real tables against each other's numbering so a "simplification" to a fixed table fails. All `TestDropInfo*` cases pass on the 6.17 x86_64 runner as well as 6.8 aarch64.
- **Fix: the controller image no longer breaks the build from a clean checkout.** The Dockerfile's web stage did not copy `package-lock.json` and ran `npm install`, which floated `@novnc/novnc` to a release whose package `exports` break vite. It now copies the lockfile and uses `npm ci`; the `web` CI job does too. This is what had `kind-e2e` red on GitHub, and it would have hit any release build.
- **CI.** New `scripts/ci-listenq-unit.sh` (minimum test count per step, `-race`, real inet_diag) in the `go` job; the `ebpf` job runs the root-only half-open case; helm asserts `NETRA_LISTEN_QUEUES`. Mutation-checked: `queue >= backlog`, never recording peaks, and not attributing half-open sockets each fail a test.

## 0.27.105 — 2026-09-19

- **CI: every test is now gated, including the ones that could silently vanish.** (1) `scripts/ci-kernel-sensors-unit.sh` gives the TCP-event and drop-attribution sensors their own unit + `-race` gate and asserts a minimum test count per step, so a renamed test cannot fall out of a `-run` filter unnoticed. (2) `scripts/ci-agent-image.sh` + a new `agent-image` job build `Dockerfile.agent` with the image's own clang and check what shipped: a BPF object for every `bpf/netra_*.c`, each a real BPF ELF, and nothing stray — a sensor added under `bpf/` but not to the Dockerfile used to build, pass every test, and ship an agent that quietly never loaded it. (3) `ci-ebpf-tests.sh` now compiles *every* `bpf/netra_*.c` instead of a hard-coded list, and adds a mutation guard: the drop-attribution program with its ethertype check removed must fail the non-IP test, proving that test still bites. (4) The `go` job also builds the whole module for darwin/arm64 and linux/arm64 (the Linux-only sensor loaders have stubs elsewhere, and releases ship arm64). (5) The `helm` job asserts the agent's tracefs mount (read-only, and absent when disabled), `NETRA_TCP_EVENTS`/`NETRA_DROP_INFO`, and the 512Mi controller memory limit. Each new gate was itself broken on purpose to confirm it fails: dropping the `COPY` of an object, a filter that matches no tests, a render without the mount, and the family-guessing mutant.
- **Kernel drop attribution: which connection, why, and which kernel function.** A new optional agent object, `bpf/netra_dropinfo.c`, attaches to `skb:kfree_skb` and records, for every dropped packet, its IP 5-tuple, the drop reason, and the address of the kernel code that dropped it (resolved through `/proc/kallsyms`, e.g. `nft_do_chain [nf_tables]`, `__udp4_lib_rcv`). The existing reason *counters* said a drop rate existed; this says whose packets and where. `GET /api/v1/ebpf/drop-info?top=&node=&reason=` (viewer) serves totals, per-reason counts, top sites and top flows tagged with their node; `/metrics` gets `netra_drop_info_*` with the reason as its only label (capped at 30, the rest in `other`). Helm `agent.dropInfo`, env `NETRA_DROP_INFO=auto|off|required`. Aggregation is in the kernel (LRU tables), not a ring buffer.
- **Reason names come from the running kernel.** Drop-reason numbering changes between kernels, so the names are parsed from the tracepoint's own `print fmt` (`tpformat` now reads `__print_symbolic` tables), not a table baked into Netra.
- **BTF only for this sensor.** The skb is read through three CO-RE-relocated `sk_buff` fields against `/sys/kernel/btf/vmlinux`. The core datapath and the other objects stay BTF-free; a kernel without BTF loses only this feature, and the agent reports why (`unavailable`) instead of just going quiet.
- **No invented tuples.** A tuple is trusted only when the tracepoint's ethertype and the IP version nibble agree; a non-IP frame, or a packet dropped before its network header was set, is counted as a drop with no tuple. Mutation-checked: making the program guess the family from the bytes fabricates `10.9.8.7 → 6.5.4.3` out of a non-IP frame and the test fails.
- **Verified on a real 6.8 kernel and verifier** (Ubuntu 24.04, clang 18): load and CO-RE relocation, a UDP datagram to a closed port attributed to the exact tuple with `NO_SOCKET`, an exact count, and `__udp4_lib_rcv`; an `nft` drop of a TCP SYN attributed to the connection with `NETFILTER_DROP`; IPv6; the non-IP case; a kernel without BTF; a layout it cannot read safely. The CI `ebpf` job runs these (they skip on a runner kernel without BTF). Not yet run in a pod or on a live node, and the per-drop cost has not been measured under a high drop rate; see `docs/drop-info.md`.

## 0.27.104 — 2026-09-19

- **TCP event tracepoints: per-connection retransmits, RSTs and the state-transition matrix.** A new optional agent object, `bpf/netra_tcpevents.c`, attaches four passive tracepoints (`tcp:tcp_retransmit_skb`, `tcp:tcp_send_reset`, `tcp:tcp_receive_reset`, `sock:inet_sock_set_state`). The existing sockops program reports retransmits only as a count at connection close and counts only `TCP_CLOSE`; this attributes retransmits and RSTs to the tuple that is losing packets *now*, in both directions, and counts every state transition. `GET /api/v1/ebpf/tcp-events` (viewer) serves the totals, the matrix and the top flows; `/metrics` gets `netra_tcp_events_*` and `netra_tcp_state_transitions{from,to}` with bounded labels. It is on by default in `auto` mode (`NETRA_TCP_EVENTS=auto|off|required`) and degrades to "not reporting" rather than failing the agent.
- **No hard-coded kernel layout.** A classic tracepoint's record layout differs between kernels and even between sibling tracepoints of one kernel (on Linux 6.8 `tcp_send_reset` has `sport` at offset 28, `tcp_receive_reset` at 16). Instead of a struct that would silently misread on some kernel, the agent reads each tracepoint's own `format` file (`internal/tpformat`), checks field names and sizes, and patches the offsets into the program's read-only data before load. A tracepoint whose layout cannot be established is left out with the reason reported; the others still run.
- **Verified on a real kernel and verifier**, not just in unit tests: Ubuntu 24.04 (Linux 6.8) with clang 18, using `bpf/integration` `TestTCPEvents*` — all four sensors load and attach, transitions are counted, received RSTs and abortive closes attributed to the right tuple, retransmits attributed under an `nft` loss rule, IPv6, missing-field and missing-tracepoint handling. The CI `ebpf` job now compiles the object, installs `nftables`, and runs these. Finding recorded in `docs/tcp-events.md`: on 6.8 `tcp_send_reset` does not fire for the kernel's RST reply to a closed port (that shows as a received RST on the dialling side).
- **Fix: the agent pod could not read tracefs, so the `kfree_skb` drop-reason sensor was silently off on real clusters.** Reading a live k3s node's agent from the host showed its `/sys` was a bare sysfs with no tracefs (the agent log said "kernel drop reason tracepoint unavailable; using stack counters only"). The DaemonSet now mounts the host's `/sys/kernel/tracing` read-only (Helm `agent.hostTracefs`, default true; also `deploy/agent.yaml`), which both this sensor and the new TCP event sensors need. On upgraded nodes the drop-reason sensor therefore starts attaching where it did not before. Helm also gets `agent.tcpEvents`.
- **Status:** the kernel object is verified on a real 6.8 kernel and verifier; the pod-level mount has been rendered (`helm template`, on and off) but this release has not yet been rolled out to the live cluster, so end-to-end reporting there is unconfirmed.

## 0.27.103 — 2026-09-19

- **Fix: the controller no longer OOM-loops under a full flow history.** On a real cluster `netrad` was OOM-killed every ~12 minutes and had been restarted 18 times in six days. The 256Mi container limit is tight for a 100k-record flow history plus 2-3 MB agent reports every few seconds, and, with no `GOMEMLIMIT`, Go's collector never reacts to the container limit: the heap runs to roughly twice its live size, the kernel kills the process, and it reloads the same state and repeats. `netrad` now reads its cgroup memory limit at startup (v2 `memory.max`, v1 `limit_in_bytes`) and sets Go's soft memory limit to 85% of it unless `GOMEMLIMIT` is set, so the collector tightens as it nears the ceiling. The chart's default controller limit is now 512Mi (request 128Mi). An operator who has set `resources.limits.memory` explicitly keeps their value.

## 0.27.102 — 2026-09-19

- **Loki push sink.** Set `NETRA_LOKI_URL` (Helm `loki.enabled`/`loki.url`) and the controller pushes audit events (including SLO burn/recovery) and block/deny events to Grafana Loki. Streams carry a small bounded label set (`job`, `class`, `severity`, `node`, plus static `NETRA_LOKI_LABELS`); the whole event is the JSON log line, queried with `| json`, so IPs, targets and actors never become high-cardinality labels. Supports tenant (`X-Scope-OrgID`), basic auth and header credentials (Helm: from an existing Secret, never values), gzip, and size-based splitting. Leader-only in HA; off by default; bad config exits at startup. Measured against a real Loki 3.7.8: 204 on success, 400 for entries older than 7 days or lines over 256 KiB (a 400 for a mixed batch still stores the valid entries), duplicates discarded. Verified end to end by `scripts/ci-loki-live.sh`, which runs the real `netrad` against a real multi-tenant Loki and reads events back through Loki's API, including recovery after a Loki outage; CI pins and checksum-verifies the Loki binary. See `docs/loki-push.md`.
- **Fix: the OTLP exporter no longer retries a rejected batch forever.** It treated every non-2xx as "retry next cycle", so a batch the collector permanently rejects (HTTP 400/413/422, which the OTLP spec says never to retry) would have wedged the log and span watermarks behind it. The delivery logic OTLP and Loki share now lives in `internal/pushfeed`: a malformed-batch status is logged with the reason and dropped, while network errors, 5xx, 429 and auth failures (401/403/404, which are fixable, so the data is kept) are still retried.

## 0.27.101 — 2026-09-19

- **Fix: per-workload metrics and SLOs, corrected against real agent data.** Deploying 0.27.100 to a live node and reading a real agent report showed three wrong assumptions. (1) Agents send *top-N snapshots*, not whole maps (flows: top 1000 by packets; connection sources: top 2000; DNS: 500; HTTP status: 1000), so a flow that merely climbs into the top N looked brand new and its whole lifetime would have been booked as one tick of growth. When a source's report is at its cap, a first sighting now only sets a baseline, and `netra_workload_report_truncated{source}` says when counts are lower bounds. The caps are now shared constants (`models.ReportCap*`) used by both the agent and the tracker. (2) Agents report a pod's direct controller, so a Deployment arrives as `ReplicaSet/checkout-6c4b4dfd7c` and changes after every rollout; an SLO for `Deployment/checkout` could never have matched. Workload names are now canonicalised (`ReplicaSet/<n>-<hash>` → `Deployment/<n>`, `Job/<n>-<minutes>` → `CronJob/<n>`), so one workload keeps one series across rollouts. (3) The agent's TCP-health list holds the *worst* sockets by retransmission score, so ratios built from it read systematically high: the `tcp_retransmit` SLI and the `netra_workload_tcp_*` series are removed rather than exported misleadingly. Live scripts now report workloads as hash-suffixed ReplicaSets, as real agents do.

## 0.27.100 — 2026-09-19

- **Per-workload metrics, network SLOs, and Prometheus Operator objects.** Three opt-in pieces on one engine. (1) `NETRA_METRICS_WORKLOAD_LABELS=on` (Helm `metrics.workloadLabels`) exports `netra_workload_<packets|bytes|blocked_packets|tcp_retransmissions|tcp_segments|connection_attempts|connections_blocked|dns_queries|dns_failures|http_responses|http_5xx>_total{namespace,workload}`. Agent counters are cumulative per flow and per node, so summing them per workload would fall whenever a pod died or an agent restarted and make `rate()` spike; a new controller-side accumulator (`internal/workloadobs`) diffs each raw entry, clamps counter resets, baselines a node's first report (and a returning node's) so history is never booked as growth, and exports true monotonic counters. Cardinality is capped (`NETRA_METRICS_WORKLOAD_MAX`, default 100): busiest workloads are admitted first and then stay put, the rest roll into one `__other__` series, and idle workloads free their slot. (2) `NETRA_SLO_DEFINITIONS` (Helm `slo.definitions`) declares SLOs over `http_5xx`, `dns_failure` or `tcp_retransmit` with multi-window burn-rate alerting; there is deliberately no latency SLI because Netra sees counters, not per-request durations. This finally wires up the previously unused `internal/slo`, extended with weighted and time-bucketed observations (so a 30-day window really covers 30 days instead of being cut at 4,096 entries), an SLI selector and a `Status()` view. `GET /api/v1/slo`, `netra_slo_*` gauges, and `slo.burn` / `slo.recovered` audit events (which flow to the SIEM, OTLP and Snowflake sinks). (3) The chart gains an opt-in `ServiceMonitor` (sends the `/metrics` token when one is set) and `PrometheusRule` (controller-health alerts, SLO alerts, series-cap alerts); the Service now carries the labels a ServiceMonitor selects on. Malformed SLO or workload config exits at startup. CI: `scripts/ci-workload-obs-unit.sh`, `scripts/ci-workload-obs-live.sh` (real `netrad` fed synthetic agent reports), and `scripts/ci-prometheus-rules.sh` (`promtool check` plus `promtool test rules` on `deploy/prometheus/netra-rules.test.yaml`, promtool pinned by checksum). Not yet run against a real Prometheus Operator or real agent data. See `docs/workload-metrics-slo.md`.

## 0.27.99 — 2026-09-19

- **OIDC login, three roles, and an optional token on `/metrics`.** Set `NETRA_OIDC_ISSUER` + `NETRA_OIDC_AUDIENCE` (Helm `auth.oidc.*`) and the API accepts IdP-issued JWTs alongside the static keys, which stay full admin as break-glass. Claims map to `viewer < operator < admin` (`NETRA_OIDC_ROLES_CLAIM`/`_ROLE_MAP`/`_DEFAULT_ROLE`). Roles are enforced centrally from the matched route pattern: viewers read and run read-only computations; operators change enforcement rules and read packet captures; admins change fleet posture, apply cluster policy, toggle features and open exec/logs/VNC consoles. Any route not listed defaults safely by method, and a test fails the build if the role table names a route that does not exist. Verification is RS/PS/ES only (`none` and `HS*` rejected), checks `iss`/`aud`/`exp`, skips weak keys, caches JWKS with a rate-limited refetch on rotation, and returns 503 (not 401) when the IdP is unreachable. Audit records now name the person (`oidc:ada@example.com`); the client-supplied `X-Netra-Actor` header can no longer override a verified identity. New `GET /api/v1/whoami` and counter `netra_rbac_denied_total`. `NETRA_METRICS_TOKEN` (Helm `auth.metricsToken`, an optional Secret key) gates `/metrics`; it stays open by default, and the OTLP exporter authenticates itself. Bad OIDC config exits at startup. Off by default. CI: `scripts/ci-auth-unit.sh`, and `scripts/ci-oidc-live.sh` drives the real `netrad` against the CI-only fake IdP `cmd/netra-ci-idp` (~40 checks incl. IdP outage and recovery). Not yet tried against a real IdP. See `docs/auth-oidc-rbac.md`.
- **CI for the OTLP push exporter.** New `scripts/ci-otlp-unit.sh` (conversion, watermarks, retry, batching, and the HA leader-only lifecycle repeated under `-race`) now runs in the `go` job.

## 0.27.98 — 2026-09-19

- **OTLP push export.** Set `NETRA_OTLP_ENDPOINT` (Helm `otlp.enabled`/`otlp.endpoint`) and the controller pushes to an OpenTelemetry collector over OTLP/HTTP JSON: metrics, audit events as logs, and block/deny events as spans. Metrics are converted from the same text `/metrics` serves (counters become monotonic cumulative sums, histograms are re-assembled), so the push cannot drift from the scrape endpoint. Logs and spans are at-least-once, deduplicated by a watermark that only advances on a successful POST (per node for block events), sent in batches of 500. Redirects count as failures instead of silently dropping data, partial-success rejections are logged, and only the HA leader pushes. Auth headers come from `NETRA_OTLP_HEADERS` (Helm: an existing Secret, key `headers`). Off by default. Unit-tested including the real `/metrics` output and the HA demote path under `-race`; not yet run against a real collector. See `docs/otlp-push.md`.
- **Zyvor Production License v1.0.** Replaced Apache-2.0. Non-production use is free. Production, SaaS, managed services, OEM, and redistribution require a separate paid commercial license (https://zyvor.dev). Source headers use `LicenseRef-Zyvor-Production-1.0`.

- **Deploy hygiene and release pipeline.** `deploy/controller.yaml` and `deploy/agent.yaml` were still pinned to `0.14.0`; they now track the current version. `scripts/check-version-sync.sh` fails CI when the version string drifts across `web/package.json`, Helm `Chart.yaml`/`values.yaml`, `internal/api/server.go`, `deploy/*.yaml`, or when `CHANGELOG.md` has no heading for it (`scripts/deploy-remote.sh` is deliberately excluded, as its hardcoded tag is self-consistent). A new `kind-e2e` CI job installs the chart into a real cluster and checks that the API answers and rejects an unauthenticated `/api/v1/status`. A new tag-triggered `release.yml` builds multi-arch controller and agent images to `ghcr.io/zyvorai`, attaches SBOM and provenance, and signs the digests with keyless cosign. `kind-e2e` and `release.yml` are linted (actionlint) but have not run on GitHub Actions yet.
- **Cleartext HTTP/1 status codes.** When a packet begins with `HTTP/1.0` or `HTTP/1.1` and a three-digit code, `http_status_stats` counts that code per cgroup. The counter is its own cgroup program, so it still loads when the SNI/Host scan programs fail the verifier. Bytes are read from the linear skb when the status line is there, and with `bpf_skb_load_bytes` at a constant TCP offset otherwise (no IP options; no TCP options, or timestamps only) so collection load does not stall the verifier. A missing cgroup id still counts. No reason phrase, no HTTP/2, no HTTP/3, no reassembly. RED exposes the 5xx total as `http5xx`. Prometheus gauge `netra_http1_status_5xx` has no pod label. Linux smoke: `scripts/ci-http-status-smoke.sh` (GitHub job `http-status-smoke`). Lab on host `NLDW4-4-16-36` counted live 200/400/502/503 responses.
- **Flow history, RED, protocol hints, process on the flow, inferred traces, kernel stacks, and pod Warning events.** `GET /api/v1/flows/history` keeps deltas for 7 days in a sidecar next to controller state (no payloads). RED, traces, profiles, workload events, and scrubbed kernel-net notes are read-only insight APIs. Prometheus gains only `netra_flowlog_records`, with no pod or destination labels. Linux smoke: `scripts/ci-flow-observe-veth.sh` (GitHub job `flow-observe-veth`). Lab on host `NLDW4-4-16-36` (2026-09-18) passed all eight steps — see `docs/flow-log.md`.
- **Drop-incident context beside auto-capture PCAPs.** A critical drop freezes `{id}.context.json` next to the PCAP: node, hostname, kernel, CPU/memory pressure, top workloads, comm-only host processes (no argv), and the drop counters already on the agent report. Download: `GET /api/v1/capture/artifacts/{id}/context`. The veth+iperf3 smoke asserts it. Lab on host `NLDW4-4-16-36` (2026-09-18), three runs, all passed — see `docs/capture.md`. Walkthrough: `docs/tutorials/drop-incident-context.md`. Peer gaps: `docs/competitive-observability.md`.
